\tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

![](images/1b7ffdcf3a86d09e9a2953e40a0196a8b8d4ea16c8d7eab29b8b93214871675a.jpg)

<details>

</details>

![](images/6702d0432117e9c2c1065da445998cb9174570bd2d519804540866ab6cf10e5a.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 1. Dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (top plot) and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (bottom plot) on $\vartheta$ for f=2 (solid curve), f=3 (dashed curve) and f=4 (dotted curve), $f=Q\cos\tau$ .   
![](images/11b6cb6bb6e89aace75756a2cc1fe8d9b6b31902b1ce08266c810ecedfead7f2.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

stage=initial_expandedrank=10query_text=In the time-averaged cross section σ̄(Q), what exact y-integrand (including differential) appears inside the 0-to-1 integral multiplying πσ0?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0point_id=cc95f23e-2c9b-5216-b908-4179dfca59a4doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4992score_vector=0.5547score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0145rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.4992score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_source=nullagent_relevance_cache_hit=nullagent_grade_cache_key=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

mically with increasing $Q$ for large $Q$ .

# III. CONCLUSION

The non-stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect is investigated in the non-relativistic approximation. Using the eikonal approach, the differential and total scattering cross sections are found. In contrast to the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect, the total cross section in the non-stationary case is finite. The main contribution to the scattering cross section comes from the angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} = \hbar\omega/E \ll 1$ . The differential scattering cross section contains an asymmetry with respect to the replacement $q \rightarrow -q$ . This asymmetry has a maximum at $\theta \sim \theta_{0}$ and decreases rapidly at $\theta \gg \theta_{0}$ and $\theta \ll \theta_{0}$ . The asymmetry in the cross section can be observed as the non-stationary Hall effect, and the total scattering cross section manifests itself in oscillations of the total electron flux.

# ACKNOWLEDGEMENT

The work of Ivan Terekhov was financially supported by the ITMO Fellowship Program.

\tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

![](images/1b7ffdcf3a86d09e9a2953e40a0196a8b8d4ea16c8d7eab29b8b93214871675a.jpg)

Figure 1. Dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (top plot) and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (bottom plot) on $\vartheta$ for f=2 (solid curve), f=3 (dashed curve) and f=4 (dotted curve), $f=Q\cos\tau$ .   
![](images/11b6cb6bb6e89aace75756a2cc1fe8d9b6b31902b1ce08266c810ecedfead7f2.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

stage=rerankrank=6query_text=In the time-averaged cross section σ̄(Q), what exact y-integrand (including differential) appears inside the 0-to-1 integral multiplying πσ0?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0point_id=cc95f23e-2c9b-5216-b908-4179dfca59a4doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.5819score_vector=0.5547score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0145rerank_score=0.7197score_composite=0.5819score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_source=nullagent_relevance_cache_hit=nullagent_grade_cache_key=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.
Hit JSON

In this case we find the asymptotics $\\Xi_{0}(y, \\tau)$ of the function $\\Xi(y, \\tau)$ ,\n\n$$\n\\Xi_ {0} (y, \\tau) = Q \\operatorname{sgn} (y) \\cos \\tau \\exp (- | y | / \\vartheta),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\vartheta = \\frac {v}{c \\zeta} = \\frac {\\theta}{\\theta_ {0}}, \\quad \\theta_ {0} = \\frac {\\hbar \\omega}{2 E}. \\tag {15}\n$$\n\nUsing integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left| s G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta} G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right| ^ {2} d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\sigma_ {0} = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P \\theta_ {0}} = \\frac {2 v}{\\pi \\omega}, \\quad \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \\cos \\tau . \\tag {16}\n$$\n\nThe function $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\\cos \\tau \\to -\\cos \\tau$ , while $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ does not change sign. Figure 1. Dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (top plot) and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (bottom plot) on $\vartheta$ for f=2 (solid curve), f=3 (dashed curve) and f=4 (dotted curve), $f=Q\cos\tau$ .   
![](images/11b6cb6bb6e89aace75756a2cc1fe8d9b6b31902b1ce08266c810ecedfead7f2.jpg)

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

!

$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

![](images/1b7ffdcf3a86d09e9a2953e40a0196a8b8d4ea16c8d7eab29b8b93214871675a.jpg)

![](images/6702d0432117e9c2c1065da445998cb9174570bd2d519804540866ab6cf10e5a.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 1. Dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (top plot) and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (bottom plot) on $\vartheta$ for f=2 (solid curve), f=3 (dashed curve) and f=4 (dotted curve), $f=Q\cos\tau$ .   
![](images/11b6cb6bb6e89aace75756a2cc1fe8d9b6b31902b1ce08266c810ecedfead7f2.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} (\vartheta \sim 1)$ . We have from (12) for $v/c \ll 1$ ,

$$
\sigma (\tau) = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {+ \infty} d y [ 1 - \cos \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ]
$$

$$
= \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {20}
$$

Naturally, there is no asymmetry in $\sigma(\tau)$ . The dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ is shown in Fig.2 for several values of Q.

!

Dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (top plot) and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)=\sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ (bottom plot) on $\vartheta$ for f=2 (solid curve), f=3 (dashed curve) and f=4 (dotted curve), $f=Q\cos\tau$ .   
![](images/11b6cb6bb6e89aace75756a2cc1fe8d9b6b31902b1ce08266c810ecedfead7f2.jpg)

<details>
<summary>line</summary>


</details>

Figure 2. Dependence of $\Sigma_{tot}(\tau)=\sigma(\tau)/\sigma_{0}$ on $\tau$ for Q=1 (solid curve), Q=2 (dashed curve) and Q=3 (dotted curve).

The total cross section increases with $Q$ and is well approximated at $Q \gg 1$ by the formula

$$
\sigma (\tau) \approx \pi \sigma_ {0} \ln (1 + 2 Q | \cos \tau |).
$$

The total cross section averaged over time may also be of interest,

$$
\overline {{\sigma}} (Q) = \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma (\tau) \frac {d \tau}{2 \pi} = \pi \sigma_ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {1} [ 1 - J _ {0} (Q y) ] \frac {d y}{y}. \tag {21}
$$

The dependence of $\overline{\Sigma}_{tot}(Q) = \overline{\sigma}(Q) / \sigma_0$ on $Q$ is shown in

Fig. 3. The time-averaged cross section grows logarith-  
![](images/edca25ca30932f8e03b27a90585e5b936b1255ad1c8b8995ae1eeba2bf1f08bc.jpg)

stage=final_outputrank=7query_text=In the time-averaged cross section σ̄(Q), what exact y-integrand (including differential) appears inside the 0-to-1 integral multiplying πσ0?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0point_id=cc95f23e-2c9b-5216-b908-4179dfca59a4doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4831score_vector=0.5547score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0145rerank_score=0.7197score_composite=0.4831score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=0.0800agent_relevance_label=irrelevantagent_relevance_source=agentagent_relevance_cache_hit=Falseagent_grade_cache_key=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0agent_relevance_keep=Trueagent_relevance_drop=Falseagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=-0.2agent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=General setup and stationary-case formulas; does not address the asked \u0304\u03c3(Q) integral.
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.
